Hello, doctor. Well... what made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I just forget lunch completely. You know, it's funny, my kitchen counter is this beautiful speckled granite... my husband, God rest his soul, installed it himself back in '98. He was always so handy like that. He built a birdhouse once that looked exactly like our old house in Ohio. We had these lovely blue jays that would visit every morning. I miss those birds. The ones around here just make such a racket. But yes, my eating is just... off.

My memory? No, I wouldn't say so. I seem to remember things just fine. Why, just this morning I remembered to water my African violets. I've had those plants for... oh, it must be five years now. My neighbor, Susan, gave me the clippings when she first moved in next door. 

Susan is such a dear, she always wears these bright purple hats when she gardens. She told me once that purple is the color of royalty. I think I read somewhere that Queen Elizabeth loved purple... or maybe it was blue. I saw this wonderful program about the royal family on the television a few nights ago. The dresses they wore back in those days were just marvelous, all that thick velvet and heavy lace. It must have been terribly hot to wear all of that in the summertime, don't you think? I can't imagine dragging all that fabric around. 

But... oh, what was the question? Right, my memory. No, I haven't really noticed any trouble with it.

Well, yes... I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this awful heavy, low feeling. It’s honestly like all the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. 

I usually put on this pink floral housecoat I have when I first get up. My sister Martha ordered it for me from one of those mail-order catalogs years ago. You just don't see those big catalogs anymore, do you? The old Sears ones used to be as thick as a telephone book. We used to use them as booster seats for the children at the dining table when they were little. That dining table... oh, it was solid oak, weighed an absolute ton. It took three men just to get it through the front door when we bought it back in '75. The delivery driver scuffed the doorframe coming in, and my husband was just fit to be tied. He had just painted that trim a crisp eggshell white the weekend before. He was so particular about his paint jobs, always used those expensive angled brushes and laid down drop cloths everywhere... 

But, um, what was I saying? Oh, yes, feeling down. Yes, it's just a very heavy, gray feeling most days.

Well, I suppose things have been slipping a bit lately. I used to be so careful with everything... but I've stopped teaching my craft classes at the community center. I just haven't had the energy. I used to teach knitting and crochet every Thursday afternoon. We made the most beautiful baby blankets for the hospital. I always liked using that soft chenille yarn, usually in a pale yellow or a mint green. Green is such a soothing color, isn't it? It reminds me of the spring when the daffodils first start poking through the frost. We had a lovely patch of daffodils by the mailbox at our old house. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, always used to compliment them. He was such a nice man, always had a pocket full of dog biscuits for all the neighborhood pups. He retired a few years back and moved to Florida, I think. A lot of people move to Florida for the weather, though I've always thought the humidity down there would be dreadful for my hair... 

Oh, but you were asking about my responsibilities, weren't you? Yes, I've been missing some phone calls and appointments, and putting off family tasks I used to handle without a second thought. I'm still managing to stay in my own home, thank goodness, but I find I have to rely on little reminder notes a lot more now, and I just put off things that used to be routine.

end up doing neither.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have a plan to hurt myself, but I do find myself thinking that if I didn’t wake up one morning, it might be a relief to everyone.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I am in that low, colorless state, I am also most caught up in the idea that the family papers and hospital notes are carrying hidden messages for me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: Looking back, the sadness and loss of interest have taken up most of this whole period since my sister became ill, even when the record worries were quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had eased enough that I was eating and getting dressed more normally, but I still felt sure the dates and names in the records were being changed to signal something.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have stopped teaching my craft classes and have missed calls, appointments, and family tasks I used to handle carefully. I still manage to stay in my home, but I rely more on reminders and put off things that used to be routine.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This whole change has been going on for about a year and five months, since my sister’s health took that serious turn.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 month\n  manifestation: The ideas about hidden messages in the family and medical papers started about a year and a month ago.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The low, colorless stretch has been with me for about a year and two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 74 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you gotten lost or confused in places that are familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, heavens no. I know my way around town just fine. I still drive myself to the grocery store every Tuesday. The big one down on Main Street... though I will say, they reorganized it a few months ago and moved the baking aisle all the way to the back, which was a bit of a nuisance. \n\nI used to love baking, you know. I'd make these wonderful peach cobblers every summer when the peaches were in season. My husband would eat half the pan in one sitting! We had a peach tree in our first backyard. It didn't yield much the first few years, but then suddenly, it just exploded with fruit. We had so many we had to give them away in little wicker baskets to the neighbors. I'd tie them up with little red ribbons... I used to buy those ribbons at the craft store that used to be right next to the post office. It's a shoe store now, I think. Or maybe a dentist's office? I haven't walked down that particular block in quite a while, it gets so breezy by the corner there. \n\nBut, um... no, to answer your question, I don't get confused about where I am. I know exactly where I'm going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day on most days?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this awful heavy, low feeling. It’s honestly like all the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. \n\nI usually put on this pink floral housecoat I have when I first get up. My sister Martha ordered it for me from one of those mail-order catalogs years ago. You just don't see those big catalogs anymore, do you? The old Sears ones used to be as thick as a telephone book. We used to use them as booster seats for the children at the dining table when they were little. That dining table... oh, it was solid oak, weighed an absolute ton. It took three men just to get it through the front door when we bought it back in '75. The delivery driver scuffed the doorframe coming in, and my husband was just fit to be tied. He had just painted that trim a crisp eggshell white the weekend before. He was so particular about his paint jobs, always used those expensive angled brushes and laid down drop cloths everywhere... \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, yes, feeling down. Yes, it's just a very heavy, gray feeling most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty managing your usual daily responsibilities, such as paying bills or handling medications?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose things have been slipping a bit lately. I used to be so careful with everything... but I've stopped teaching my craft classes at the community center. I just haven't had the energy. I used to teach knitting and crochet every Thursday afternoon. We made the most beautiful baby blankets for the hospital. I always liked using that soft chenille yarn, usually in a pale yellow or a mint green. Green is such a soothing color, isn't it? It reminds me of the spring when the daffodils first start poking through the frost. We had a lovely patch of daffodils by the mailbox at our old house. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, always used to compliment them. He was such a nice man, always had a pocket full of dog biscuits for all the neighborhood pups. He retired a few years back and moved to Florida, I think. A lot of people move to Florida for the weather, though I've always thought the humidity down there would be dreadful for my hair... \n\nOh, but you were asking about my responsibilities, weren't you? Yes, I've been missing some phone calls and appointments, and putting off family tasks I used to handle without a second thought. I'm still managing to stay in my own home, thank goodness, but I find I have to rely on little reminder notes a lot more now, and I just put off things that used to be routine.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave any family members or friends noticed changes in your memory or thinking?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my sister Martha has said a few things. She tells me she just can’t follow what I mean lately. Like the other day, I was trying to explain my schedule and some appointments I had coming up, or maybe I was trying to explain my genealogy notes... I've been doing a lot of family history research lately. It's so fascinating digging into the past. I was looking for our grandmother’s birth certificate. She was born in 1898, and back then they wrote everything out by hand at the courthouse. The county clerk had the most beautiful cursive handwriting. You just don't see penmanship like that anymore. They don't even teach cursive in the schools now, which is a crying shame if you ask me. How are children supposed to read historical documents? Or sign a check? Though I suppose everybody just uses those little plastic cards now. My nephew has one that he just taps on the machine at the grocery store, doesn't even have to put it in or type a number. It's like magic, but I don't trust it one bit. Anyone could just walk by and tap your card! 

But, oh, what was I saying about Martha? Right, so I was trying to explain the genealogy notes, but I had started off talking about her medicine list—she takes a lot of water pills for her blood pressure, you see—and then suddenly I’m talking about our grandmother's birth certificate and the county clerk, and Martha just threw her hands up. She said I was jumping all over the place and she couldn't understand how I got from her pharmacy to the 1890s. So... yes, I suppose people have noticed my thoughts getting a bit jumbled when I try to talk.

She tells me she just can’t follow what I mean lately. Like the other day, I was trying to explain my schedule and some appointments I had coming up, or maybe I was trying to explain my genealogy notes... I've been doing a lot of family history research lately. It's so fascinating digging into the past. I was looking for our grandmother’s birth certificate. She was born in 1898, and back then they wrote everything out by hand at the courthouse. The county clerk had the most beautiful cursive handwriting. You just don't see penmanship like that anymore. They don't even teach cursive in the schools now, which is a crying shame if you ask me. How are children supposed to read historical documents? Or sign a check? Though I suppose everybody just uses those little plastic cards now. My nephew has one that he just taps on the machine at the grocery store, doesn't even have to put it in or type a number. It's like magic, but I don't trust it one bit. Anyone could just walk by and tap your card! \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about Martha? Right, so I was trying to explain the genealogy notes, but I had started off talking about her medicine list—she takes a lot of water pills for her blood pressure, you see—and then suddenly I’m talking about our grandmother's birth certificate and the county clerk, and Martha just threw her hands up. She said I was jumping all over the place and she couldn't understand how I got from her pharmacy to the 1890s. So... yes, I suppose people have noticed my thoughts getting a bit jumbled when I try to talk.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Which started first? Oh, my... it really feels like it all just sort of happened together, to be honest. This whole change has been going on for about a year and five months, right around the time my sister’s health took that serious turn. 

Martha, I mean. She had to be in the hospital for a bit, and they put her in this room on the fourth floor. The view was just dreadful, looking right out over the top of the parking garage. I don't know why they build those garages to be so terribly ugly. Just big, gray blocks of concrete everywhere. My late husband used to say concrete was the most unforgiving material in the world to work with. He poured our patio out back in the summer of '82. It was a terribly hot summer, just sweltering, and I had to keep bringing him tall pitchers of sweet iced tea while he worked. I always put a sprig of fresh mint from the garden in my iced tea. Mint is lovely, but it grows like an absolute weed if you aren't careful. It will take over your whole flowerbed before you can even blink if you don't keep it in a pot... 

I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, which came first. Yes, I really couldn't separate the mood from the jumbled thoughts. It all just started about a year and five months ago when Martha got so sick.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think... yes, there was a time recently when my mood did lift a bit. There was a stretch of several weeks where I wasn't feeling quite so heavy and sad. I was actually getting dressed in the mornings instead of staying in my robe, and I was eating my meals more normally. I even made myself a nice pot roast one Sunday. I always use the chuck roast, it gets so tender if you cook it low and slow. My mother taught me to always sear the meat first to lock in the flavor, and then you add carrots and potatoes and just a little bit of beef broth. She used to use those big, thick carrots straight from Mr. Miller's farm stand down the road. He always had the sweetest corn in the late summer, too. We’d buy a whole bushel and sit on the back porch shucking it. The silks would get everywhere, just sticking to your clothes and blowing in the wind...

Oh, but you were asking about my thoughts, weren't you? Yes, even during those few weeks when I was feeling better and eating my pot roast, my thoughts were still... well, they were still troubling me. I was still doing my genealogy research, but I felt absolutely sure that the dates and names in the historical records were being changed. It felt like someone was altering them on purpose to signal something to me. Even though I wasn't feeling as sad, I still couldn't shake that feeling about the records being tampered with.

It will take over your whole flowerbed before you can even blink if you don't keep it in a pot... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, which came first. Yes, I really couldn't separate the mood from the jumbled thoughts. It all just started about a year and five months ago when Martha got so sick.\n\nDoctor: On days when you feel less sad, do you still have the same difficulty organizing your thoughts and managing everyday tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... yes, there was a time recently when my mood did lift a bit. There was a stretch of several weeks where I wasn't feeling quite so heavy and sad. I was actually getting dressed in the mornings instead of staying in my robe, and I was eating my meals more normally. I even made myself a nice pot roast one Sunday. I always use the chuck roast, it gets so tender if you cook it low and slow. My mother taught me to always sear the meat first to lock in the flavor, and then you add carrots and potatoes and just a little bit of beef broth. She used to use those big, thick carrots straight from Mr. Miller's farm stand down the road. He always had the sweetest corn in the late summer, too. We’d buy a whole bushel and sit on the back porch shucking it. The silks would get everywhere, just sticking to your clothes and blowing in the wind...\n\nOh, but you were asking about my thoughts, weren't you? Yes, even during those few weeks when I was feeling better and eating my pot roast, my thoughts were still... well, they were still troubling me. I was still doing my genealogy research, but I felt absolutely sure that the dates and names in the historical records were being changed. It felt like someone was altering them on purpose to signal something to me. Even though I wasn't feeling as sad, I still couldn't shake that feeling about the records being tampered with.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you still believe that someone is deliberately changing the historical records to send you messages?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. I know it sounds odd to say it out loud, but I still feel it very strongly. I keep looking at our family papers, and I just get this feeling that certain dates and names are being changed, or even hidden from me on purpose. It's like... well, like the hospital where Martha stayed, or maybe even someone in our own family, is trying to send me little messages through those records. 

Speaking of the family, my cousin Arthur used to be the one who kept all the records. He lived in this big, drafty Victorian house over on Elm Street. The woodwork in that house was just magnificent, solid oak everywhere you looked, but the heating bills must have been astronomical. He had this old grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every quarter hour. It was so loud, I don't know how he ever got a wink of sleep. I stayed there once when I was a girl, during a terrible snowstorm in '58. The snow was up to my waist, and we couldn't go to school for three days. We just sat by the fire and roasted chestnuts. I haven't had a roasted chestnut in years... they're so hard to find nowadays. You used to be able to buy them from a man with a cart downtown, right by the old department store... 

But anyway, yes, the records. I still believe someone is altering them to send me messages. It's very unsettling.

I've been doing a lot of family history research lately. It's so fascinating digging into the past. I was looking for our grandmother’s birth certificate. She was born in 1898, and back then they wrote everything out by hand at the courthouse. The county clerk had the most beautiful cursive handwriting. You just don't see penmanship like that anymore. They don't even teach cursive in the schools now, which is a crying shame if you ask me. How are children supposed to read historical documents? Or sign a check? Though I suppose everybody just uses those little plastic cards now. My nephew has one that he just taps on the machine at the grocery store, doesn't even have to put it in or type a number. It's like magic, but I don't trust it one bit. Anyone could just walk by and tap your card! \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about Martha? Right, so I was trying to explain the genealogy notes, but I had started off talking about her medicine list—she takes a lot of water pills for her blood pressure, you see—and then suddenly I’m talking about our grandmother's birth certificate and the county clerk, and Martha just threw her hands up. She said I was jumping all over the place and she couldn't understand how I got from her pharmacy to the 1890s. Oh, my... it really feels like it all just sort of happened together, to be honest. She had to be in the hospital for a bit, and they put her in this room on the fourth floor. The view was just dreadful, looking right out over the top of the parking garage. I don't know why they build those garages to be so terribly ugly. Just big, gray blocks of concrete everywhere. My late husband used to say concrete was the most unforgiving material in the world to work with. He poured our patio out back in the summer of '82. It was a terribly hot summer, just sweltering, and I had to keep bringing him tall pitchers of sweet iced tea while he worked. I always put a sprig of fresh mint from the garden in my iced tea. Mint is lovely, but it grows like an absolute weed if you aren't careful. It will take over your whole flowerbed before you can even blink if you don't keep it in a pot... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, which came first. There was a stretch of several weeks where I wasn't feeling quite so heavy and sad. I was actually getting dressed in the mornings instead of staying in my robe, and I was eating my meals more normally. I even made myself a nice pot roast one Sunday. I always use the chuck roast, it gets so tender if you cook it low and slow. My mother taught me to always sear the meat first to lock in the flavor, and then you add carrots and potatoes and just a little bit of beef broth. She used to use those big, thick carrots straight from Mr. Miller's farm stand down the road. He always had the sweetest corn in the late summer, too. We’d buy a whole bushel and sit on the back porch shucking it. I was still doing my genealogy research, but I felt absolutely sure that the dates and names in the historical records were being changed. It felt like someone was altering them on purpose to signal something to me. Even though I wasn't feeling as sad, I still couldn't shake that feeling about the records being tampered with.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that someone is deliberately changing the historical records to send you messages?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Worsened? Oh, I suppose they have, yes. It's just so frustrating lately. I'll sit there staring at simple things, trying to figure out whether to call the pharmacy or open the mail first. 

Speaking of the mail, the new postman—Gary, I think his name is—he wears those little shorts even when it's practically freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't just lock up. My knees ache terribly when the damp weather sets in. My old doctor used to tell me to take a hot bath with Epsom salts. You can buy the lavender-scented ones now, but I always preferred the plain. Lavender gives me a bit of a headache if it's too strong. I had a huge lavender bush by the front steps years ago, and the bees just swarmed it all summer long. You couldn't even sit on the porch swing without worrying about getting stung, especially in August. August is always the worst month for yellow jackets... 

Anyway, where was I? Oh, the fogginess. Yes, I'll be looking at the mail or the phone, and my mind just gets so incredibly foggy. I get so stuck trying to decide what to do that I just end up doing neither of them. So yes, I do think it's been getting steadily worse.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well... what made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I just forget lunch completely. You know, it's funny, my kitchen counter is this beautiful speckled granite... my husband, God rest his soul, installed it himself back in '98. He was always so handy like that. He built a birdhouse once that looked exactly like our old house in Ohio. We had these lovely blue jays that would visit every morning. I miss those birds. The ones around here just make such a racket. But yes, my eating is just... off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed problems with your memory recently?\n\nPatient: My memory? No, I wouldn't say so. I seem to remember things just fine. Why, just this morning I remembered to water my African violets. I've had those plants for... oh, it must be five years now. My neighbor, Susan, gave me the clippings when she first moved in next door. \n\nSusan is such a dear, she always wears these bright purple hats when she gardens. She told me once that purple is the color of royalty. I think I read somewhere that Queen Elizabeth loved purple... or maybe it was blue. I saw this wonderful program about the royal family on the television a few nights ago. The dresses they wore back in those days were just marvelous, all that thick velvet and heavy lace. It must have been terribly hot to wear all of that in the summertime, don't you think? I can't imagine dragging all that fabric around. \n\nBut... oh, what was the question? Right, my memory. No, I haven't really noticed any trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten lost or confused in places that are familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, heavens no. I know my way around town just fine. I still drive myself to the grocery store every Tuesday. The big one down on Main Street... though I will say, they reorganized it a few months ago and moved the baking aisle all the way to the back, which was a bit of a nuisance. \n\nI used to love baking, you know. I'd make these wonderful peach cobblers every summer when the peaches were in season. My husband would eat half the pan in one sitting! We had a peach tree in our first backyard. It didn't yield much the first few years, but then suddenly, it just exploded with fruit. We had so many we had to give them away in little wicker baskets to the neighbors. I'd tie them up with little red ribbons... I used to buy those ribbons at the craft store that used to be right next to the post office. It's a shoe store now, I think. Or maybe a dentist's office? I haven't walked down that particular block in quite a while, it gets so breezy by the corner there. \n\nBut, um... no, to answer your question, I don't get confused about where I am. I know exactly where I'm going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day on most days?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this awful heavy, low feeling. It’s honestly like all the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. \n\nI usually put on this pink floral housecoat I have when I first get up. My sister Martha ordered it for me from one of those mail-order catalogs years ago. You just don't see those big catalogs anymore, do you? The old Sears ones used to be as thick as a telephone book. We used to use them as booster seats for the children at the dining table when they were little. That dining table... oh, it was solid oak, weighed an absolute ton. It took three men just to get it through the front door when we bought it back in '75. The delivery driver scuffed the doorframe coming in, and my husband was just fit to be tied. He had just painted that trim a crisp eggshell white the weekend before. He was so particular about his paint jobs, always used those expensive angled brushes and laid down drop cloths everywhere... \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, yes, feeling down. Yes, it's just a very heavy, gray feeling most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty managing your usual daily responsibilities, such as paying bills or handling medications?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose things have been slipping a bit lately. I used to be so careful with everything... but I've stopped teaching my craft classes at the community center. I just haven't had the energy. I used to teach knitting and crochet every Thursday afternoon. We made the most beautiful baby blankets for the hospital. I always liked using that soft chenille yarn, usually in a pale yellow or a mint green. Green is such a soothing color, isn't it? It reminds me of the spring when the daffodils first start poking through the frost. We had a lovely patch of daffodils by the mailbox at our old house. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, always used to compliment them. He was such a nice man, always had a pocket full of dog biscuits for all the neighborhood pups. He retired a few years back and moved to Florida, I think. A lot of people move to Florida for the weather, though I've always thought the humidity down there would be dreadful for my hair... \n\nOh, but you were asking about my responsibilities, weren't you? Yes, I've been missing some phone calls and appointments, and putting off family tasks I used to handle without a second thought. I'm still managing to stay in my own home, thank goodness, but I find I have to rely on little reminder notes a lot more now, and I just put off things that used to be routine.\n\nDoctor: Have any family members or friends noticed changes in your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: Well, my sister Martha has said a few things. She tells me she just can’t follow what I mean lately. Like the other day, I was trying to explain my schedule and some appointments I had coming up, or maybe I was trying to explain my genealogy notes... I've been doing a lot of family history research lately. It's so fascinating digging into the past. I was looking for our grandmother’s birth certificate. She was born in 1898, and back then they wrote everything out by hand at the courthouse. The county clerk had the most beautiful cursive handwriting. You just don't see penmanship like that anymore. They don't even teach cursive in the schools now, which is a crying shame if you ask me. How are children supposed to read historical documents? Or sign a check? Though I suppose everybody just uses those little plastic cards now. My nephew has one that he just taps on the machine at the grocery store, doesn't even have to put it in or type a number. It's like magic, but I don't trust it one bit. Anyone could just walk by and tap your card! \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about Martha? Right, so I was trying to explain the genealogy notes, but I had started off talking about her medicine list—she takes a lot of water pills for her blood pressure, you see—and then suddenly I’m talking about our grandmother's birth certificate and the county clerk, and Martha just threw her hands up. She said I was jumping all over the place and she couldn't understand how I got from her pharmacy to the 1890s. So... yes, I suppose people have noticed my thoughts getting a bit jumbled when I try to talk.\n\nDoctor: Which started first: the problems with your thinking and daily tasks, or the persistent low mood?\n\nPatient: Which started first? Oh, my... it really feels like it all just sort of happened together, to be honest. This whole change has been going on for about a year and five months, right around the time my sister’s health took that serious turn. \n\nMartha, I mean. She had to be in the hospital for a bit, and they put her in this room on the fourth floor. The view was just dreadful, looking right out over the top of the parking garage. I don't know why they build those garages to be so terribly ugly. Just big, gray blocks of concrete everywhere. My late husband used to say concrete was the most unforgiving material in the world to work with. He poured our patio out back in the summer of '82. It was a terribly hot summer, just sweltering, and I had to keep bringing him tall pitchers of sweet iced tea while he worked. I always put a sprig of fresh mint from the garden in my iced tea. Mint is lovely, but it grows like an absolute weed if you aren't careful. It will take over your whole flowerbed before you can even blink if you don't keep it in a pot... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, which came first. Yes, I really couldn't separate the mood from the jumbled thoughts. It all just started about a year and five months ago when Martha got so sick.\n\nDoctor: On days when you feel less sad, do you still have the same difficulty organizing your thoughts and managing everyday tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... yes, there was a time recently when my mood did lift a bit. There was a stretch of several weeks where I wasn't feeling quite so heavy and sad. I was actually getting dressed in the mornings instead of staying in my robe, and I was eating my meals more normally. I even made myself a nice pot roast one Sunday. I always use the chuck roast, it gets so tender if you cook it low and slow. My mother taught me to always sear the meat first to lock in the flavor, and then you add carrots and potatoes and just a little bit of beef broth. She used to use those big, thick carrots straight from Mr. Miller's farm stand down the road. He always had the sweetest corn in the late summer, too. We’d buy a whole bushel and sit on the back porch shucking it. The silks would get everywhere, just sticking to your clothes and blowing in the wind...\n\nOh, but you were asking about my thoughts, weren't you? Yes, even during those few weeks when I was feeling better and eating my pot roast, my thoughts were still... well, they were still troubling me. I was still doing my genealogy research, but I felt absolutely sure that the dates and names in the historical records were being changed. It felt like someone was altering them on purpose to signal something to me. Even though I wasn't feeling as sad, I still couldn't shake that feeling about the records being tampered with.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that someone is deliberately changing the historical records to send you messages?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I know it sounds odd to say it out loud, but I still feel it very strongly. I keep looking at our family papers, and I just get this feeling that certain dates and names are being changed, or even hidden from me on purpose. It's like... well, like the hospital where Martha stayed, or maybe even someone in our own family, is trying to send me little messages through those records. \n\nSpeaking of the family, my cousin Arthur used to be the one who kept all the records. He lived in this big, drafty Victorian house over on Elm Street. The woodwork in that house was just magnificent, solid oak everywhere you looked, but the heating bills must have been astronomical. He had this old grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every quarter hour. It was so loud, I don't know how he ever got a wink of sleep. I stayed there once when I was a girl, during a terrible snowstorm in '58. The snow was up to my waist, and we couldn't go to school for three days. We just sat by the fire and roasted chestnuts. I haven't had a roasted chestnut in years... they're so hard to find nowadays. You used to be able to buy them from a man with a cart downtown, right by the old department store... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the records. I still believe someone is altering them to send me messages. It's very unsettling.\n\nDoctor: Have your thinking difficulties steadily worsened over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Worsened? Oh, I suppose they have, yes. It's just so frustrating lately. I'll sit there staring at simple things, trying to figure out whether to call the pharmacy or open the mail first. \n\nSpeaking of the mail, the new postman—Gary, I think his name is—he wears those little shorts even when it's practically freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't just lock up. My knees ache terribly when the damp weather sets in. My old doctor used to tell me to take a hot bath with Epsom salts. You can buy the lavender-scented ones now, but I always preferred the plain. Lavender gives me a bit of a headache if it's too strong. I had a huge lavender bush by the front steps years ago, and the bees just swarmed it all summer long. You couldn't even sit on the porch swing without worrying about getting stung, especially in August. August is always the worst month for yellow jackets... \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, the fogginess. Yes, I'll be looking at the mail or the phone, and my mind just gets so incredibly foggy. I get so stuck trying to decide what to do that I just end up doing neither of them. So yes, I do think it's been getting steadily worse.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It's very unsettling.\n\nDoctor: Have your thinking difficulties steadily worsened over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Worsened? Oh, I suppose they have, yes. It's just so frustrating lately. I'll sit there staring at simple things, trying to figure out whether to call the pharmacy or open the mail first. \n\nSpeaking of the mail, the new postman—Gary, I think his name is—he wears those little shorts even when it's practically freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't just lock up. My knees ache terribly when the damp weather sets in. My old doctor used to tell me to take a hot bath with Epsom salts. You can buy the lavender-scented ones now, but I always preferred the plain. Lavender gives me a bit of a headache if it's too strong. I had a huge lavender bush by the front steps years ago, and the bees just swarmed it all summer long. You couldn't even sit on the porch swing without worrying about getting stung, especially in August. August is always the worst month for yellow jackets... \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, the fogginess. Yes, I'll be looking at the mail or the phone, and my mind just gets so incredibly foggy. I get so stuck trying to decide what to do that I just end up doing neither of them. So yes, I do think it's been getting steadily worse.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you made any mistakes paying your bills or managing your finances because you were confused or forgot what to do?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Bills? Oh, well... I suppose I haven't made any terrible mistakes with the money, but I have been putting things off. I used to handle all of those things so carefully, you know. I'd sit down at the kitchen table every Sunday evening with my checkbook and a nice cup of tea. Earl Grey, usually. I always liked the bergamot in it, it smells so comforting. I've missed some calls and appointments, and family tasks I used to be so on top of. I even stopped teaching my craft classes at the community center. I just... I couldn't keep it all organized anymore. So things that used to be routine, I just put them off now.

She was born in 1898, and back then they wrote everything out by hand at the courthouse. The county clerk had the most beautiful cursive handwriting. You just don't see penmanship like that anymore. They don't even teach cursive in the schools now, which is a crying shame if you ask me. How are children supposed to read historical documents? Or sign a check? He was so particular about his paint jobs, always used those expensive angled brushes and laid down drop cloths everywhere... \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, yes, feeling down. Yes, it's just a very heavy, gray feeling most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty managing your usual daily responsibilities, such as paying bills or handling medications?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose things have been slipping a bit lately. I used to be so careful with everything... but I've stopped teaching my craft classes at the community center. I just haven't had the energy. I used to teach knitting and crochet every Thursday afternoon. We made the most beautiful baby blankets for the hospital. I always liked using that soft chenille yarn, usually in a pale yellow or a mint green. Green is such a soothing color, isn't it? It reminds me of the spring when the daffodils first start poking through the frost. We had a lovely patch of daffodils by the mailbox at our old house. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, always used to compliment them. He was such a nice man, always had a pocket full of dog biscuits for all the neighborhood pups. He retired a few years back and moved to Florida, I think. A lot of people move to Florida for the weather, though I've always thought the humidity down there would be dreadful for my hair... \n\nOh, but you were asking about my responsibilities, weren't you? Yes, I've been missing some phone calls and appointments, and putting off family tasks I used to handle without a second thought. I'm still managing to stay in my own home, thank goodness, but I find I have to rely on little reminder notes a lot more now, and I just put off things that used to be routine.\n\nDoctor: Have any family members or friends noticed changes in your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: Well, my sister Martha has said a few things. She tells me she just can’t follow what I mean lately. Like the other day, I was trying to explain my schedule and some appointments I had coming up, or maybe I was trying to explain my genealogy notes... I've been doing a lot of family history research lately. It's so fascinating digging into the past. I was looking for our grandmother’s birth certificate. She was born in 1898, and back then they wrote everything out by hand at the courthouse. The county clerk had the most beautiful cursive handwriting. You just don't see penmanship like that anymore. They don't even teach cursive in the schools now, which is a crying shame if you ask me. How are children supposed to read historical documents? Or sign a check? Though I suppose everybody just uses those little plastic cards now. My nephew has one that he just taps on the machine at the grocery store, doesn't even have to put it in or type a number. It's like magic, but I don't trust it one bit. Anyone could just walk by and tap your card! \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about Martha? Right, so I was trying to explain the genealogy notes, but I had started off talking about her medicine list—she takes a lot of water pills for her blood pressure, you see—and then suddenly I’m talking about our grandmother's birth certificate and the county clerk, and Martha just threw her hands up. She said I was jumping all over the place and she couldn't understand how I got from her pharmacy to the 1890s. So... yes, I suppose people have noticed my thoughts getting a bit jumbled when I try to talk.\n\nDoctor: Which started first: the problems with your thinking and daily tasks, or the persistent low mood?\n\nPatient: Which started first? Oh, my... it really feels like it all just sort of happened together, to be honest. This whole change has been going on for about a year and five months, right around the time my sister’s health took that serious turn. \n\nMartha, I mean. She had to be in the hospital for a bit, and they put her in this room on the fourth floor. The view was just dreadful, looking right out over the top of the parking garage. I don't know why they build those garages to be so terribly ugly. Just big, gray blocks of concrete everywhere. My late husband used to say concrete was the most unforgiving material in the world to work with. He poured our patio out back in the summer of '82. It was a terribly hot summer, just sweltering, and I had to keep bringing him tall pitchers of sweet iced tea while he worked. I always put a sprig of fresh mint from the garden in my iced tea. Mint is lovely, but it grows like an absolute weed if you aren't careful. It will take over your whole flowerbed before you can even blink if you don't keep it in a pot... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, which came first. Yes, I really couldn't separate the mood from the jumbled thoughts. It all just started about a year and five months ago when Martha got so sick.\n\nDoctor: On days when you feel less sad, do you still have the same difficulty organizing your thoughts and managing everyday tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... yes, there was a time recently when my mood did lift a bit. There was a stretch of several weeks where I wasn't feeling quite so heavy and sad. I was actually getting dressed in the mornings instead of staying in my robe, and I was eating my meals more normally. I even made myself a nice pot roast one Sunday. I always use the chuck roast, it gets so tender if you cook it low and slow. My mother taught me to always sear the meat first to lock in the flavor, and then you add carrots and potatoes and just a little bit of beef broth. She used to use those big, thick carrots straight from Mr. Miller's farm stand down the road. He always had the sweetest corn in the late summer, too. We’d buy a whole bushel and sit on the back porch shucking it. The silks would get everywhere, just sticking to your clothes and blowing in the wind...\n\nOh, but you were asking about my thoughts, weren't you? Yes, even during those few weeks when I was feeling better and eating my pot roast, my thoughts were still... well, they were still troubling me. I was still doing my genealogy research, but I felt absolutely sure that the dates and names in the historical records were being changed. It felt like someone was altering them on purpose to signal something to me. Even though I wasn't feeling as sad, I still couldn't shake that feeling about the records being tampered with.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that someone is deliberately changing the historical records to send you messages?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I know it sounds odd to say it out loud, but I still feel it very strongly. I keep looking at our family papers, and I just get this feeling that certain dates and names are being changed, or even hidden from me on purpose. It's like... well, like the hospital where Martha stayed, or maybe even someone in our own family, is trying to send me little messages through those records. \n\nSpeaking of the family, my cousin Arthur used to be the one who kept all the records. He lived in this big, drafty Victorian house over on Elm Street. The woodwork in that house was just magnificent, solid oak everywhere you looked, but the heating bills must have been astronomical. He had this old grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every quarter hour. It was so loud, I don't know how he ever got a wink of sleep. I stayed there once when I was a girl, during a terrible snowstorm in '58. The snow was up to my waist, and we couldn't go to school for three days. We just sat by the fire and roasted chestnuts. I haven't had a roasted chestnut in years... they're so hard to find nowadays. You used to be able to buy them from a man with a cart downtown, right by the old department store... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the records. I still believe someone is altering them to send me messages. It's very unsettling.\n\nDoctor: Have your thinking difficulties steadily worsened over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Worsened? Oh, I suppose they have, yes. It's just so frustrating lately. I'll sit there staring at simple things, trying to figure out whether to call the pharmacy or open the mail first. \n\nSpeaking of the mail, the new postman—Gary, I think his name is—he wears those little shorts even when it's practically freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't just lock up. My knees ache terribly when the damp weather sets in. My old doctor used to tell me to take a hot bath with Epsom salts. You can buy the lavender-scented ones now, but I always preferred the plain. Lavender gives me a bit of a headache if it's too strong. I had a huge lavender bush by the front steps years ago, and the bees just swarmed it all summer long. You couldn't even sit on the porch swing without worrying about getting stung, especially in August. August is always the worst month for yellow jackets... \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, the fogginess. Yes, I'll be looking at the mail or the phone, and my mind just gets so incredibly foggy. I get so stuck trying to decide what to do that I just end up doing neither of them. So yes, I do think it's been getting steadily worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you made any mistakes paying your bills or managing your finances because you were confused or forgot what to do?\n\nPatient: Bills? Oh, well... I suppose I haven't made any terrible mistakes with the money, but I have been putting things off. I used to handle all of those things so carefully, you know. I'd sit down at the kitchen table every Sunday evening with my checkbook and a nice cup of tea. Earl Grey, usually. I always liked the bergamot in it, it smells so comforting. I used to buy the loose leaf tea from a little shop downtown, right next to the bakery that sold those wonderful almond croissants. The lady who ran the tea shop—Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was—she always had a little bowl of peppermints on the counter. She had the most beautiful porcelain teapots in the window, all hand-painted with little roses and vines. I bought one for my sister once, for her anniversary. She kept it on a high shelf in her dining room so the cat wouldn't knock it over. That cat was a menace, always climbing the curtains... \n\nBut, oh, you were asking about my routine. Yes, I still manage to stay in my home, but I rely so much more on little reminders now. I've missed some calls and appointments, and family tasks I used to be so on top of. I even stopped teaching my craft classes at the community center. I just... I couldn't keep it all organized anymore. So things that used to be routine, I just put them off now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Well, when my mood picked up... yes, there was a stretch of several weeks where the heaviness lifted a bit. I was actually eating regular meals again, and I started getting dressed in the mornings instead of just staying in my robe. My favorite robe is this thick, terrycloth one I bought at Sears years ago. It's a lovely shade of pale blue. They don't make them like that anymore, the ones you buy now are so flimsy, they fall apart after three washes. I remember the day I bought it, it was raining cats and dogs, and I had to park all the way at the end of the lot. By the time I got inside, my shoes were completely soaked through. I was wearing those little leather loafers, the ones with the tassels? They were never quite the same after that. Water just ruins good leather, you know. You have to treat it with mink oil, but I never remember to do that until it's too late...

But anyway, even during those weeks when I was feeling a bit better and doing my normal things... I still couldn't shake that feeling about the records. I was still absolutely sure that the dates and names were being changed to signal something to me. The mood was better, but that... that feeling never went away.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF03.92, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well... what made me come in is that my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I just forget lunch completely. And other days, I'll just stand at the kitchen counter eating crackers and sweets, even though nothing really tastes right. You know, it's funny, my kitchen counter is this beautiful speckled granite... my husband, God rest his soul, installed it himself back in '98. He was always so handy like that. He built a birdhouse once that looked exactly like our old house in Ohio. We had these lovely blue jays that would visit every morning. I miss those birds. The ones around here just make such a racket. But yes, my eating is just... off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed problems with your memory recently?\n\nPatient: My memory? No, I wouldn't say so. I seem to remember things just fine. Why, just this morning I remembered to water my African violets. I've had those plants for... oh, it must be five years now. My neighbor, Susan, gave me the clippings when she first moved in next door. \n\nSusan is such a dear, she always wears these bright purple hats when she gardens. She told me once that purple is the color of royalty. I think I read somewhere that Queen Elizabeth loved purple... or maybe it was blue. I saw this wonderful program about the royal family on the television a few nights ago. The dresses they wore back in those days were just marvelous, all that thick velvet and heavy lace. It must have been terribly hot to wear all of that in the summertime, don't you think? I can't imagine dragging all that fabric around. \n\nBut... oh, what was the question? Right, my memory. No, I haven't really noticed any trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten lost or confused in places that are familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, heavens no. I know my way around town just fine. I still drive myself to the grocery store every Tuesday. The big one down on Main Street... though I will say, they reorganized it a few months ago and moved the baking aisle all the way to the back, which was a bit of a nuisance. \n\nI used to love baking, you know. I'd make these wonderful peach cobblers every summer when the peaches were in season. My husband would eat half the pan in one sitting! We had a peach tree in our first backyard. It didn't yield much the first few years, but then suddenly, it just exploded with fruit. We had so many we had to give them away in little wicker baskets to the neighbors. I'd tie them up with little red ribbons... I used to buy those ribbons at the craft store that used to be right next to the post office. It's a shoe store now, I think. Or maybe a dentist's office? I haven't walked down that particular block in quite a while, it gets so breezy by the corner there. \n\nBut, um... no, to answer your question, I don't get confused about where I am. I know exactly where I'm going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day on most days?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I suppose I have. Most mornings I wake up with this awful heavy, low feeling. It’s honestly like all the color has just gone out of the day before I even get dressed. \n\nI usually put on this pink floral housecoat I have when I first get up. My sister Martha ordered it for me from one of those mail-order catalogs years ago. You just don't see those big catalogs anymore, do you? The old Sears ones used to be as thick as a telephone book. We used to use them as booster seats for the children at the dining table when they were little. That dining table... oh, it was solid oak, weighed an absolute ton. It took three men just to get it through the front door when we bought it back in '75. The delivery driver scuffed the doorframe coming in, and my husband was just fit to be tied. He had just painted that trim a crisp eggshell white the weekend before. He was so particular about his paint jobs, always used those expensive angled brushes and laid down drop cloths everywhere... \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, yes, feeling down. Yes, it's just a very heavy, gray feeling most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty managing your usual daily responsibilities, such as paying bills or handling medications?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose things have been slipping a bit lately. I used to be so careful with everything... but I've stopped teaching my craft classes at the community center. I just haven't had the energy. I used to teach knitting and crochet every Thursday afternoon. We made the most beautiful baby blankets for the hospital. I always liked using that soft chenille yarn, usually in a pale yellow or a mint green. Green is such a soothing color, isn't it? It reminds me of the spring when the daffodils first start poking through the frost. We had a lovely patch of daffodils by the mailbox at our old house. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, always used to compliment them. He was such a nice man, always had a pocket full of dog biscuits for all the neighborhood pups. He retired a few years back and moved to Florida, I think. A lot of people move to Florida for the weather, though I've always thought the humidity down there would be dreadful for my hair... \n\nOh, but you were asking about my responsibilities, weren't you? Yes, I've been missing some phone calls and appointments, and putting off family tasks I used to handle without a second thought. I'm still managing to stay in my own home, thank goodness, but I find I have to rely on little reminder notes a lot more now, and I just put off things that used to be routine.\n\nDoctor: Have any family members or friends noticed changes in your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: Well, my sister Martha has said a few things. She tells me she just can’t follow what I mean lately. Like the other day, I was trying to explain my schedule and some appointments I had coming up, or maybe I was trying to explain my genealogy notes... I've been doing a lot of family history research lately. It's so fascinating digging into the past. I was looking for our grandmother’s birth certificate. She was born in 1898, and back then they wrote everything out by hand at the courthouse. The county clerk had the most beautiful cursive handwriting. You just don't see penmanship like that anymore. They don't even teach cursive in the schools now, which is a crying shame if you ask me. How are children supposed to read historical documents? Or sign a check? Though I suppose everybody just uses those little plastic cards now. My nephew has one that he just taps on the machine at the grocery store, doesn't even have to put it in or type a number. It's like magic, but I don't trust it one bit. Anyone could just walk by and tap your card! \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying about Martha? Right, so I was trying to explain the genealogy notes, but I had started off talking about her medicine list—she takes a lot of water pills for her blood pressure, you see—and then suddenly I’m talking about our grandmother's birth certificate and the county clerk, and Martha just threw her hands up. She said I was jumping all over the place and she couldn't understand how I got from her pharmacy to the 1890s. So... yes, I suppose people have noticed my thoughts getting a bit jumbled when I try to talk.\n\nDoctor: Which started first: the problems with your thinking and daily tasks, or the persistent low mood?\n\nPatient: Which started first? Oh, my... it really feels like it all just sort of happened together, to be honest. This whole change has been going on for about a year and five months, right around the time my sister’s health took that serious turn. \n\nMartha, I mean. She had to be in the hospital for a bit, and they put her in this room on the fourth floor. The view was just dreadful, looking right out over the top of the parking garage. I don't know why they build those garages to be so terribly ugly. Just big, gray blocks of concrete everywhere. My late husband used to say concrete was the most unforgiving material in the world to work with. He poured our patio out back in the summer of '82. It was a terribly hot summer, just sweltering, and I had to keep bringing him tall pitchers of sweet iced tea while he worked. I always put a sprig of fresh mint from the garden in my iced tea. Mint is lovely, but it grows like an absolute weed if you aren't careful. It will take over your whole flowerbed before you can even blink if you don't keep it in a pot... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, which came first. Yes, I really couldn't separate the mood from the jumbled thoughts. It all just started about a year and five months ago when Martha got so sick.\n\nDoctor: On days when you feel less sad, do you still have the same difficulty organizing your thoughts and managing everyday tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... yes, there was a time recently when my mood did lift a bit. There was a stretch of several weeks where I wasn't feeling quite so heavy and sad. I was actually getting dressed in the mornings instead of staying in my robe, and I was eating my meals more normally. I even made myself a nice pot roast one Sunday. I always use the chuck roast, it gets so tender if you cook it low and slow. My mother taught me to always sear the meat first to lock in the flavor, and then you add carrots and potatoes and just a little bit of beef broth. She used to use those big, thick carrots straight from Mr. Miller's farm stand down the road. He always had the sweetest corn in the late summer, too. We’d buy a whole bushel and sit on the back porch shucking it. The silks would get everywhere, just sticking to your clothes and blowing in the wind...\n\nOh, but you were asking about my thoughts, weren't you? Yes, even during those few weeks when I was feeling better and eating my pot roast, my thoughts were still... well, they were still troubling me. I was still doing my genealogy research, but I felt absolutely sure that the dates and names in the historical records were being changed. It felt like someone was altering them on purpose to signal something to me. Even though I wasn't feeling as sad, I still couldn't shake that feeling about the records being tampered with.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that someone is deliberately changing the historical records to send you messages?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I know it sounds odd to say it out loud, but I still feel it very strongly. I keep looking at our family papers, and I just get this feeling that certain dates and names are being changed, or even hidden from me on purpose. It's like... well, like the hospital where Martha stayed, or maybe even someone in our own family, is trying to send me little messages through those records. \n\nSpeaking of the family, my cousin Arthur used to be the one who kept all the records. He lived in this big, drafty Victorian house over on Elm Street. The woodwork in that house was just magnificent, solid oak everywhere you looked, but the heating bills must have been astronomical. He had this old grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every quarter hour. It was so loud, I don't know how he ever got a wink of sleep. I stayed there once when I was a girl, during a terrible snowstorm in '58. The snow was up to my waist, and we couldn't go to school for three days. We just sat by the fire and roasted chestnuts. I haven't had a roasted chestnut in years... they're so hard to find nowadays. You used to be able to buy them from a man with a cart downtown, right by the old department store... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the records. I still believe someone is altering them to send me messages. It's very unsettling.\n\nDoctor: Have your thinking difficulties steadily worsened over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Worsened? Oh, I suppose they have, yes. It's just so frustrating lately. I'll sit there staring at simple things, trying to figure out whether to call the pharmacy or open the mail first. \n\nSpeaking of the mail, the new postman—Gary, I think his name is—he wears those little shorts even when it's practically freezing out. I don't know how his knees don't just lock up. My knees ache terribly when the damp weather sets in. My old doctor used to tell me to take a hot bath with Epsom salts. You can buy the lavender-scented ones now, but I always preferred the plain. Lavender gives me a bit of a headache if it's too strong. I had a huge lavender bush by the front steps years ago, and the bees just swarmed it all summer long. You couldn't even sit on the porch swing without worrying about getting stung, especially in August. August is always the worst month for yellow jackets... \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, the fogginess. Yes, I'll be looking at the mail or the phone, and my mind just gets so incredibly foggy. I get so stuck trying to decide what to do that I just end up doing neither of them. So yes, I do think it's been getting steadily worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you made any mistakes paying your bills or managing your finances because you were confused or forgot what to do?\n\nPatient: Bills? Oh, well... I suppose I haven't made any terrible mistakes with the money, but I have been putting things off. I used to handle all of those things so carefully, you know. I'd sit down at the kitchen table every Sunday evening with my checkbook and a nice cup of tea. Earl Grey, usually. I always liked the bergamot in it, it smells so comforting. I used to buy the loose leaf tea from a little shop downtown, right next to the bakery that sold those wonderful almond croissants. The lady who ran the tea shop—Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was—she always had a little bowl of peppermints on the counter. She had the most beautiful porcelain teapots in the window, all hand-painted with little roses and vines. I bought one for my sister once, for her anniversary. She kept it on a high shelf in her dining room so the cat wouldn't knock it over. That cat was a menace, always climbing the curtains... \n\nBut, oh, you were asking about my routine. Yes, I still manage to stay in my home, but I rely so much more on little reminders now. I've missed some calls and appointments, and family tasks I used to be so on top of. I even stopped teaching my craft classes at the community center. I just... I couldn't keep it all organized anymore. So things that used to be routine, I just put them off now.\n\nDoctor: During the several weeks when your mood improved, were you still having difficulty managing everyday tasks such as appointments, medications, or household responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, when my mood picked up... yes, there was a stretch of several weeks where the heaviness lifted a bit. I was actually eating regular meals again, and I started getting dressed in the mornings instead of just staying in my robe. My favorite robe is this thick, terrycloth one I bought at Sears years ago. It's a lovely shade of pale blue. They don't make them like that anymore, the ones you buy now are so flimsy, they fall apart after three washes. I remember the day I bought it, it was raining cats and dogs, and I had to park all the way at the end of the lot. By the time I got inside, my shoes were completely soaked through. I was wearing those little leather loafers, the ones with the tassels? They were never quite the same after that. Water just ruins good leather, you know. You have to treat it with mink oil, but I never remember to do that until it's too late...\n\nBut anyway, even during those weeks when I was feeling a bit better and doing my normal things... I still couldn't shake that feeling about the records. I was still absolutely sure that the dates and names were being changed to signal something to me. The mood was better, but that... that feeling never went away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I used to buy the loose leaf tea from a little shop downtown, right next to the bakery that sold those wonderful almond croissants. The lady who ran the tea shop—Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was—she always had a little bowl of peppermints on the counter. She had the most beautiful porcelain teapots in the window, all hand-painted with little roses and vines. I bought one for my sister once, for her anniversary. She kept it on a high shelf in her dining room so the cat wouldn't knock it over. That cat was a menace, always climbing the curtains... \n\nBut, oh, you were asking about my routine. Yes, I still manage to stay in my home, but I rely so much more on little reminders now. I've missed some calls and appointments, and family tasks I used to be so on top of. I even stopped teaching my craft classes at the community center. I just... I couldn't keep it all organized anymore. So things that used to be routine, I just put them off now.\n\nDoctor: During the several weeks when your mood improved, were you still having difficulty managing everyday tasks such as appointments, medications, or household responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, when my mood picked up... yes, there was a stretch of several weeks where the heaviness lifted a bit. I was actually eating regular meals again, and I started getting dressed in the mornings instead of just staying in my robe. My favorite robe is this thick, terrycloth one I bought at Sears years ago. It's a lovely shade of pale blue. They don't make them like that anymore, the ones you buy now are so flimsy, they fall apart after three washes. I remember the day I bought it, it was raining cats and dogs, and I had to park all the way at the end of the lot. By the time I got inside, my shoes were completely soaked through. I was wearing those little leather loafers, the ones with the tassels? They were never quite the same after that. Water just ruins good leather, you know. You have to treat it with mink oil, but I never remember to do that until it's too late...\n\nBut anyway, even during those weeks when I was feeling a bit better and doing my normal things... I still couldn't shake that feeling about the records. I was still absolutely sure that the dates and names were being changed to signal something to me. The mood was better, but that... that feeling never went away."
